There were blood transfusions , another stem cell transplant , from an unrelated donor , more chemo , more setbacks . She went into remission again , relapsed , joined another clinical trial and contracted a form of the Epstein-Barr virus . The donated cells attacked her own , a condition called graft-versus-host disease . When she came home after a stint in the hospital in October , she was too weak to pick up her children . <p> Her oncologist told her that he thought he could , maybe , keep her alive for another year . <p> " For my whole life , I have tried to be good , " she wrote , " to be a good student and a good sister and a good daughter , and to protect my mother and never make her upset or angry . Caroline Kennedy , a former ambassador to Australia and Japan , was just five when her father was assassinated on November 22nd , 1963 ; she was 10 when her uncle Robert F Kennedy , a presidential candidate in the Democratic primary of 1968 , was murdered . She was 24 , and they thought she might blend in . <p> " Even when I was in college , I had never really wanted to go to parties , " she wrote , " and now I was going to have to spend a Friday night trying to find out where parties were at a school where I did n't go and did n't know anyone ? Nightmarish . " <p> She continued , " Also , no one had ever really offered me drugs in college , so I had no idea how I was supposed to find them . @@132895129 <h> It must be time for a ' mast year ' for humanity , when we decide not to give up , but to rise again to the challenge <p> The global tide of environmental consciousness keeps coming in and going out . It rose first when Rachel Carson called out the hubris of the pesticide industries in her book Silent Spring . The tide was pulled higher as we first saw the Earth rise , looking back from the moon . The movement gained momentum from the anti-war , civil rights , gay and feminist campaigns of the 1960s and 70s and became mainstream thanks to the power of television , recording the destruction of our natural world . <p> Green parties sprang up everywhere in the early 1980s , just when the wider public interest in the environment seemed to temporarily fade . Margaret Thatcher and Ronald Reagan dominated the new era instead . Greed was good again , government was bad , shoulder pads were in and hippies were very much out . <p> That changed in the late 80s , perhaps because we discovered a hole @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the first time . I started canvassing door to door around that time . It was like picking apples from the bottom of the tree . That resurgence in public interest led to the establishment of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change at the Earth Summit in Rio in 1992 . This was a high water mark . <p> It seemed we were at last on course to address the ecological crisis , only for political support to once again waiver . Bill Clinton and Tony Blair promoted a new third way in politics , where the election mantra was " it 's the economy , stupid " . US Congress killed the Kyoto agreement at birth and the environmental movement was left to encourage better consumer behaviour , change being all about how you get the markets to turn things around . <p> Al Gore single-handedly changed things far more with his seminal film An Inconvenient Truth in 2006 , only for the financial crash to suck the life out of any multilateral co-operation , as could be seen the disastrous COP 15 in Copenhagen in 2009 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ again . Ever more alarming reports from climate scientists , along with the publication of the Laudato Si encyclical by Pope Francis , gave impetus for the world to agree the sustainable development goals and the Paris climate agreement in the autumn of 2015 . The climate strikes organised by Greta Thunberg then helped give legislators a real mandate to act . <p> Once again , however , it was only a temporary watershed moment . A combination of the affordability crisis following the war in Ukraine , a green backlash pumped up on social media and resistance from those with a vested interest in the status quo , all weakened public support for action . <p> From a green perspective 2025 must have been one of the darkest years ever . Cop30 in Belem showed a lack of leadership at the international level . Trump 's assault on climate action will surely be seen in the future as a crime against humanity and nature . Populist politics in Europe were little better , with environmental ambition being falsely depicted as part of some cultural war or as being punitively @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be competitive or secure , when we continue relying on burning other people 's fossil fuels . <p> Here at home , the abandonment of funding for nature restoration would break your heart and the lack of priority for public transport would wreck your head as the tailbacks grow on the N7 , the M50 and beyond . <p> We 've lost half of the natural world in the past 50 years and greenhouse gas emissions continue to rise . Our survival is threatened but that no longer makes the news . Have we just given up ? Should we just accept the pollution in our rivers and shrug our shoulders at the increasing numbers of pedestrians and cyclists being hit ? Do we see the destruction of the natural world as an inevitable price for some immediate material gain we are all chasing after ? Or will the tide turn again and a new wave of environmental thinking arrive just in time ? <p> One feature of the past year might give us some hope and inspiration . 2025 was a fantastic example of what is called a " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the oak , ash and beech produce a super abundance of acorns , seeds and nuts , like they are throwing everything they have at their future survival . <p> Surely it must be time for a " mast year " for humanity , when we decide not to give up but to rise again to the challenge , knowing that the solutions we bring will work better for everyone . It is never too late to act and make a lasting difference . <p> It helps if you take a long-term perspective , understanding that the tide of ecological consciousness may come in and go out , but the sea level of green thinking is still on the rise . 
@@132895315 <h> Peatland viruses playing ' active role ' in ecosystem health - research <p> Viruses found in peatlands could play a more important role in carbon storage than previously thought and become indicators of ecosystem health , according to a study . <p> Scientists from the Universities of Edinburgh and Aberdeen in Scotland and Wisconsin -- Madison in the US examined viruses in peatland soils from 66 locations across seven UK peatland sites , comparing natural , damaged and restored areas . <p> Their analysis revealed that viruses behaved in a similar way in each type of peatland , adapting their behavior based on the stability of the habitat . <h> Carbon storage <p> Changes in viral behavior affects the dynamics of the soil 's microbiome , influencing which bacterium are present and how efficiently bacteria can process and store carbon , the experts said . <p> Peatlands , like other types of wetlands , play a crucial role in storing carbon that would otherwise remain in the atmosphere and contribute to global warming . <p> Previous research has shown that despite covering only 3% of the Earth 's surface @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ global soil carbon due to their oxygen poor conditions that drastically slows down the decomposition and release of carbon into the atmosphere . <p> However , these fragile ecosystems are under increasing threat from environmental disturbances including climate change , drainage and damage caused by land use , the researchers said . <p> Some areas are even switching from being carbon sinks to carbon sources . <h> Ecosystems <p> The scientists found that in natural peatlands , viruses were able to infect cells of microbes , replicate and then kill the cell before leaving to infect other cells . <p> This keeps microbe populations in check and the ecosystem relatively stable . <p> In damaged peatlands , they found that the viruses opt to hunker down in infected cells after replicating rather than immediately killing them . <p> Through looking at which viruses were present in a soil sample , researchers could determine if a wetland was natural , has been damaged or if restoration efforts are working . <p> The team believes the patterns of viruses observed across UK peatlands could be applicable on a global scale and as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ could be useful tools to influence the restoration of peatlands . <p> The study , published in Nature Microbiology , was funded by UKRI Natural Environment Research Council , Scottish Universities Partnership for Environmental Research and the National Science Foundation . <h> Future research <p> Since microbes including viruses play a key role in regulating greenhouse gas fluxes , " our results suggest that viruses can act as signals of peatland recovery by providing a window into what 's happening below-ground " , Dr. Ashish Malik , co-senior author and Chancellor 's Fellow in Climate and Environmental Sustainability , University of Edinburgh said . <p> Lead author James Kosmopoulos , a PhD student at the University of Wisconsin , said that viruses are " like a keystone predator in the microbial world " . <p> " Without them , everything in an ecosystem would be out of whack , " he said . <p> Meanwhile , Kartik Anantharama , associate professor of bacteriology at the University of Wisconsin , said that bacterial processes produce carbon dioxide and methane from this carbon-heavy , peatland soil . <p> The professor added : @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do it , and are there any other components that we have missed out on ? And that 's where viruses come in. "

@@132909798 <h> " While the carbon footprint has been falling , the overall level of emissions has been increasing . " - Prof Gerry Boyle <p> Since its inception , Bord Bia 's Sustainable Dairy Assurance Scheme ( SDAS ) has provided a focus on some of the key metrics of climate change , especially the so-called carbon footprint , or the kg amount of CO2-e per kg of fat corrected milk and protein ( FCMP ) . <p> SDAS also provides an indication of the key relationships between the estimated footprint and key @ for overall emissions to be increasing due to an increase in production that offsets the reduction in the footprint . <p> Production , in turn , will be driven by cow numbers and feed and fertiliser consumption . <p> SDAS is not the only source of information on climate change metrics in Irish farming . <p> Teagasc has been using its National Farm Survey data to generate similar metrics to SDAS the preceding five years . @@132926013 <h> Social enterprises can be powerful drivers of climate action <p> In an era of increasingly severe storms , social enterprises are reusing , repurposing , and recycling resources in innovative ways -- while also reinvesting back into their social cause <p> President Catherine Connolly working with instructor Cian Corcoran assembling a cabinet during her visit to Common Knowledge , the award-winning social enterprise in Co Clare earlier this month . Picture : Eamon Ward <p> Storm Bram earlier this month again reminded us that our island of Ireland is especially vulnerable to extreme weather patterns . We are all too aware of how Ireland 's climate is changing but it has n't translated into action beyond grim statements , finger pointing , and blame . <p> Yes , Ireland has astutely declared a climate emergency and , yes , there are policies to achieve a 51% reduction in greenhouse emissions by 2030 , but we find ourselves in the troubling nowhere land between climate action and climate inaction . <p> Storms such as Floris , Bert , Darragh , ? owyn , and most recently Bram with their intense @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ brought the future to us in the present . <p> Whether we accept it or not , Ireland is experiencing more storms more regularly . <p> We have been bombarded with images of and stories about recent storms that have shown in the most stark way just how damaging extreme climate events can be to our society , our economy , and our environment . <p> About 40% of the Irish population lives within a few kilometres of the coast -- you do n't need to be an expert to see the risks to homes , businesses , and infrastructure as storm activity increases . We only have to ask ourselves how we would cope without electricity at home for weeks on end or with knowing that our livelihoods have been severely impacted or even lost . <p> So how can we respond ? <p> Climate resilience <p> Much has been written about the important role of science in modelling prediction around storms and rising seas . This is not in doubt but there is so much more to be done . <p> Policymakers need to continue to translate this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ resilience that clearly recognises the many ways climate change disproportionately affects the most vulnerable . <p> Small business can lead the way <p> While the damage caused by storms is something we see and are shocked by , one area that often gets overlooked that is quietly leading the way in climate action is business -- small business . <p> Social entrepreneurs are innovating through business ventures to create and sustain social change and impact . They may be hiding in plain sight insofar as we know there are around 4,300 operating in Ireland . They employ 3% of the Irish population and have a combined income of ? 2.34bn . <p> Their impact must be observed firsthand in the local context to understand why they are so important . Social entrepreneurs provide opportunities to the long-term unemployed , people with disabilities , migrants , the Traveller community , and former offenders . <p> Graham Dwyer is an associate professor at Trinity Business School and co-director of Trinity Centre for Social Innovation . <p> These are the invisible armies known locally to be playing a crucial role in making Ireland @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , concurrent , and compounded inequalities that are accentuated by climate injustices . <p> Social entrepreneurs and their employees are driven by a mission to achieve social and environmental good by selling goods and services that enable them to invest back into causes , which has been the basis for the formation of these social enterprises in the first place . <p> They can exist as intermediaries in the ways that they connect , facilitate , capacity-build , and bridge gaps that private , public , and non-profit sector organisations struggle to plug . <p> In many ways , the impact of social enterprises highlights the failures of big businesses , which often fail in their responsibilities to reduce their carbon emissions . <p> Business for good <p> Simply put , social enterprises reuse , repurpose , and recycle resources in innovative ways that are moving the needle towards climate action . <p> While lowering carbon emissions , they also transform business for good by prioritising impact while reinvesting back into their social cause . They educate , facilitate , and support communities to tackle climate change as well as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not hold back the seas and storms that come with climate change , we do have control over how we respond . <p> Social enterprises lead the way by showing how a low-carbon society can be achieved that collectively enables us all to be part of solutions in the climate action discussion , rather than being part of the problem . <p> The most dangerous place to be is in the nowhere land between climate action and inaction , where we avoid the difficult discussion about who is accountable and responsible -- the inconvenient truth is , we all are . <p> * Graham Dwyer is an associate professor at Trinity Business School and co-director of Trinity Centre for Social Innovation , Trinity College Dublin . He is the author of Making Sense of Natural Disasters ( Palgrave Macmillan ) .

@@132926394 <h> Grant available for archaeological monuments in county Cork <p> The 2026 Community Monuments Fund will enable conservation works to be carried out on archaeological monuments deemed to be significant and in need of support . <p> Cork County Council is inviting private owners , custodians , and community groups to apply for funding to support the care , conservation , maintenance , protection , and promotion of archaeological monuments in their area . <p> Cork County Council is inviting private owners , custodians , and community groups to apply for funding to support the care , conservation , maintenance , protection , and promotion of archaeological monuments in their area . <p> The 2026 Community Monuments Fund will enable conservation works to be carried out on archaeological monuments deemed to be significant and in need of support . ? 7.5 million has been made available nationally for the scheme in 2026 . <p> The fund aims to build resilience in monuments , enabling them to withstand the effects of climate change , to encourage access to monuments and improve their presentation . <p> The scheme has three streams of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of eligible costs and include conservation works , conservation management plans and enhanced access and interpretation as well as emergency works . <p> Mayor of the County of Cork , Mary Linehan Foley said : " County Cork has a rich archaeological legacy , with almost 20,000 recorded monuments across the County . <p> " Through the Community Monuments Fund , vital support has been provided in recent years for a diverse range of important archaeological projects . I strongly encourage owners and groups to apply under the 2026 programme . " <p> Chief executive of Cork County Council , Moira Murrell added : " The Community Monuments Fund is an initiative of the Department of Housing , Local Government and Heritage and has become an important avenue for supporting and promoting archaeological heritage across County Cork . <p> " The scheme is administered at the local level by Cork County Council 's Heritage Unit and continues to attract strong interest each year , reflecting the high level of engagement and commitment to heritage protection throughout the County . @@132926821 <h> Chris Hinchliff MP : Delivering genuine results for North East Herts <p> This article is brought to you by our exclusive subscriber partnership with our sister title USA Today , and has been written by our American colleagues . It does not necessarily reflect the view of The Herald . <p> As we come to the end of 2025 , I want to take a moment to look back on what has been an incredibly busy and rewarding year . <p> Representing our community is a privilege , and every success we have achieved together , whether it is saving a local school from closure , improving transport links , or shaping national policy , has come from listening to you and fighting for what matters most . <p> This year has been about getting things done . Practical wins that make life better for people here at home , and national changes that will help families across the country . Here are just some of the highlights : <h> Sandon JMI <p> A few weeks ago , Sandon JMI School was faced with the very real threat of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is not just about education , it is about losing a vital part of village life . <p> When I heard about the consultation , I wrote to Hertfordshire County Council calling on them to pause the process so we could make the case for keeping the school open . <p> I am pleased to say that a stay of execution was granted , giving us the breathing space we need to fight for its future . <h> Royston Banking Hub <p> My constituents tell me that access to banking services is essential , especially for older residents and small businesses . <p> That is why I pushed so hard for a Banking Hub in Royston , and this year I had the honour of cutting the ribbon at its new permanent site . This hub means everyone , regardless of age or digital skills , can access face-to-face banking in our town . <p> Leaks in Letchworth Sometimes , the issues that matter most are the ones right outside your front door . After weeks of delays on Glebe Road in Letchworth , I wrote to Affinity Water demanding @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ responsibility and fixed the problem shortly after . Whether it 's about dealing with a specific issue on your street , or calling out rogue practices in the House of Commons chamber , I will always stand up for you over vested corporate interests . <h> Faster Rail for Commuters <p> I have been pressing Govia Thameslink Railway for improvements , and I am delighted that the major East Coast Main Line timetable change will deliver faster , more frequent peak services from Cambridge to London King 's Cross . This means more seats , quicker journeys , and a better experience for everyone who relies on these routes . <h> Jess 's Rule <p> At national level , Jessica Brady 's tragic death from missed cancer diagnoses highlighted a serious gap in our healthcare system . <p> Working with Jess 's family , I engaged with the Department of Health and NHS England to push for change . <p> The result is Jess 's Rule , a new initiative that prompts GPs to think again if a patient presents the same unresolved symptoms after three appointments . It is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <h> Hugh 's Law <p> When a child is diagnosed with a life-limiting illness , parents should be able to focus on care , not worry about financial ruin . <p> That is why I have been working with my constituents Ceri and Frances Menai-Davis to champion Hugh 's Law , which calls for day-one support for parents of seriously ill children . <p> I am proud that Hugh 's Law is set to have its own dedicated section in the Carer 's Review consultation . This is about dignity , compassion , and giving families the support they need at the hardest time of their lives . <h> Standing Up for Nature <p> And Britain would not be Britain without its natural heritage , so I 'm pleased that after campaigning so hard with the UK 's nature and wildlife charities , we have secured new protections for precious chalk streams in national planning policy , as well as a change in legislation so that new policies must deliver overall improvements to irreplaceable habitats like ancient woodland . <p> Other work in this area means we 'll also see @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ there are proposals for hedgehog highways and swift bricks to feature in most new developments too . <p> Alongside these achievements , there are important issues that remain in progress : <h> The A505 <p> Road safety on the A505 is a major concern for many residents , and I have engaged with Hertfordshire County Council , Hertfordshire Constabulary and the Department for Transport on this matter . <p> I plan to bring all the relevant parties together in the new year to find cost-effective solutions that can be implemented quickly . Too many lives have been impacted by this stretch of road and action is long overdue . <h> Better Buses <p> Local bus services are another priority . I have been calling for improvements to rural routes and the Government has now given Hertfordshire County Council new powers and funding to deliver better services that put local people first . Earlier this year I surveyed constituents to understand what residents want from their bus network , and I will keep pressing the council to deliver on those expectations . <h> Local Healthcare <p> Healthcare provision remains a pressing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ raise concerns about the future of Royston Hospital and to stress the need for local voices in any decision-making . I also raised the plans for a new healthcare centre in Letchworth . I will keep engaging with the department to make sure outcomes meet the needs of our communities . <p> Rural Crime Finally , tackling rural crime is high on my agenda . I am engaging with Hertfordshire Constabulary on issues ranging from illegal trail hunting and hare coursing to river pollution , fly-tipping and the destruction of habitats for rare species . <p> These crimes damage our environment and threaten the character of our countryside . I will continue to push for stronger enforcement and practical solutions . <h> More To Come <p> Looking ahead to 2026 , there are many more issues I will remain focussed on . <p> Three in particular come to mind : First , with a white paper on SEND reform due in early 2026 , I will be amplifying the views of local parents to ensure any changes truly work for families and guarantee every child gets the support they deserve @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Chris Hinchliff MP ) <p> Second , I will continue pushing to secure sustainable funding for our local hospices , Garden House and Isabel , so they can continue providing brilliant compassionate care to those who need it most . And third , I remain committed to defending local democracy in our planning system and pushing for more action to tackle climate change and restore nature across our countryside . <p> Thank you for your support and engagement throughout the year . Together , we have shown that when we speak up and stand together , we can make real change happen . <h> Follow Us <h> Useful Links <p> This website and associated newspapers adhere to the Independent Press Standards Organisation 's Editors ' Code of Practice .
